_source line 154 · 2026-05-16T04:13:16.469Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have a thorough understanding of the entire paper. Let me compile my review based on the ARR review form criteria.

Let me summarize what I've read:

**Paper Summary:**
The paper introduces "ordinal depth estimation" - classifying text into generation depth classes (gen-0/gen-1/gen-2+) rather than binary human/machine detection, in the context of non-paraphrastic LLM continuation chains. The paper characterizes a "differential depth response" where central-tendency and diversity features drift monotonically with depth while shape features remain stationary. A 7-feature ensemble achieves binary F1=0.723 for gen-1 vs gen-2+ discrimination. The paper also reports a negative finding that depth-aware data curation doesn't improve downstream LM quality.
 
The paper's main strengths are its novel task formulation addressing a real gap in detection, rigorous experimental methodology with proper generalization testing, and the compelling empirical finding about how text properties shift systematically across generation depths. The authors deserve credit for honestly reporting negative results about downstream utility and thoroughly documenting their work through extensive ablations and sound statistical practices like bootstrap confidence intervals and multiple comparison corrections.

However, there's a fundamental tension between the scope of claims and the actual scope of findings. The restriction to base models severely undermines practical relevance since instruction-tuned models—which dominate real-world deployment—show dramatically degraded performance on the depth detection task. This gap between what the paper claims to address (non-paraphrastic LLM text chains in general) and what it actually demonstrates (base model chains) isn't adequately flagged in the framing. Additionally, the experimental design choice to use shared seed texts for generation-0 across all generators introduces potential artifacts that could confound the depth signal measurements.

The evaluation also relies heavily on a single prompt type in the main results, with cross-prompt robustness only partially explored on a single model. The 3-class LOGO evaluation that's prominently reported appears inflated due to the train-test overlap on generation-0 texts, and the paper acknowledges but doesn't fully address how this affects the interpretation of depth discrimination claims.

There's a potential confound with the reference model choice—Pythia-410M shares architecture with two of the held-out generators, and those Pythia models happen to perform best, suggesting an unexamined interaction between reference and generator families. The entropy_min feature contributes almost nothing to performance but is kept anyway.

The downstream experiment is quite limited in scope, using only a single model, single task, and around 4K training samples.  The perplexity gap between random and depth-stratified sampling is negligible with minimal variance across runs, yet the conclusion that the diagnostic isn't curative is stated broadly despite this narrow evidence base.

The practical F1 score of 0.723 translates to roughly 28% of depth assignments being incorrect, which is substantial for a diagnostic tool. While individual generators range from 0.52 to 0.81, the ensemble's per-generator standard deviation doesn't clarify the actual range of performance, and there's no clear context for what error rate would be acceptable in real decision-making scenarios.

The XGBoost hyperparameter choices differ significantly between the ensemble (200 estimators, depth 6) and baselines (100, depth 3), and while sensitivity analysis shows minimal impact, this asymmetry should be justified upfront rather than validated post-hoc. Bootstrap confidence intervals use 1000 resamples, but the paper doesn't specify whether they're percentile, BCa, or normal-approximation—this distinction matters because F1 distributions can be skewed, especially under class imbalance. The location-shift hypothesis framing presents the correlation between depth and feature values as mechanistic, but it's purely observational without establishing causality.

The paper controls for topic narrowing (<9% variance) but doesn't fully exclude other confounds like prompt-induced distributional bias. Table 3 reports per-generator binary F1 with std=0.051, yet doesn't show the individual values—the 3-class per-generator range (0.683 to 0.856) is much wider than this standard deviation would suggest, so a full breakdown would clarify consistency. The cross-model chain evaluation uses 5-fold stratified CV rather than LOGO, which differs from the main evaluation protocol and complicates the claim that depth signals persist across heterogeneous chain provenance.

The 4-detector ensemble has 4 features while the 7-feature ensemble has 7, and when surface features are added to the 4-detector setup (reaching 6 features), performance matches the 7-feature ensemble—suggesting the gap is partly attributable to feature count rather than feature quality alone. The Table 3 caption claims the 7-feature ensemble is strongest on both metrics, but the 4-detector + surface row actually shows higher 3-class F1 (0.805 vs 0.789) and equivalent binary F1, so the wording needs adjustment. Figure 1's bar chart for Spearman correlations...

The bar chart obscures how performance varies across individual generators; error bars or plotting each generator separately would be clearer. The justification for the tail_decay 90th percentile threshold cites Hendrycks & Gimpel 2017 as "standard practice in heavy-tail analysis," but that paper focuses on OOD detection rather than heavy-tail analysis specifically, so the citation's relevance should be stated more precisely. The reference verification flagged 6 missing entries and 2 mismatches (mostly recent papers not yet indexed in OpenAlex), though the bibliography entries themselves appear correct when checked manually. There's also an arithmetic inconsistency: the paper mentions roughly 1,000 seed passages per generator and approximately 27,000 texts total across all generators and depths, which would suggest around 6 generators × 1,000 seeds × 5 depths ≈ 30,000, yet the confusion matrix shows N=27,529—this gap should be clarified.

Now I'm formulating specific questions for the authors about the per-generator F1 scores, the bootstrap confidence interval methodology, whether there's an interaction between reference model and generator family, how the Wikipedia passages were sampled, whether downstream results were significance-tested with confidence intervals, and a discrepancy in the Qwen3-0.6B F1 score between the Limitations section and Table 1. I also notice a reference to Guo et al. that appears incomplete.

Let me add the missing citations—there's a 2025 paper on training data effects in detection, a 2024 paper on multi-hop fact generation, and the baseline detector datasets should be cited. Before I finalize the review, I should double-check the key numerical claims: the abstract's binary F1 of 0.723 with its confidence interval matches Table 3, the 3-class LOGO F1 of 0.789 aligns with Table 1, the per-generator standard deviation of 0.051 is in Table 3, and I need to verify the Bonferroni correction details.

Now I'm spot-checking all the reported metrics—the QLoRA improvement of 11.6 percentage points over the baseline, the 5-class F1 score, the downstream task comparisons, cross-model chain results, and the LOFO importance scores across different models. I'm noticing something doesn't quite add up.

There's a discrepancy between Table 4's Pythia-410M LOGO F1 of 0.803 and Table 1's reported 6-gen LOGO average of 0.789. Looking more closely, the 5-gen LOGO result in Table 1 is 0.802, which matches Table 4 almost exactly. So it seems like Table 4 might actually be reporting 5-gen LOGO results rather than 6-gen, which would explain the 0.014 difference.

The Discussion section also mentions multiple generator splits—a 6-gen LOGO setup and a 7-gen setup that includes an instruct variant. Table 4's cross-reference results appear to use a different generator split than the main Table 1 results, which is creating these inconsistencies that need clarification. I'm noticing the cross-reference table values don't cleanly map to either the 5-gen or 6-gen LOGO results from the earlier tables, which suggests there might be some ambiguity about which evaluation setup produced these numbers. The Qwen3-0.6B result is particularly interesting because using it as a reference model while also having it as one of the evaluated generators raises questions about whether the feature computation would be affected by model family overlap. I'm noticing a key issue: when Qwen3-0.6B serves as the reference model, computing NLL on its own generated text creates a self-referencing problem that could skew results differently than for other generators. Looking at the numbers more carefully, the 0.816 cross-ref result for Qwen3-0.6B represents the LOGO average across all generators, which is distinct from the 0.824 held-out F1 in Table 1. The real discrepancy I need to understand is why Table 4's Pythia-410M LOGO F1 (0.803) differs from Table 1's 6-generator LOGO average (0.789)—this likely stems from Table 4 using 5-generator LOGO while Table 1 uses 6-generator LOGO, which is a confusing inconsistency.

The Discussion section clarifies that the 0.816 figure comes from a 7-generator experiment (6 base models plus 1 instruct variant) where Qwen3-0.6B base achieved that score, while the 0.824 in Table 1 is from the 6-generator base-only setup—so these are consistent, though the matching 0.816 in Table 4 for cross-ref is likely just coincidence. This numbering confusion significantly impacts readability.

Stepping back to evaluate the paper holistically: it presents a well-motivated contribution with clear task formulation and solid experimental methodology, but the practical limitations are substantial—the approach only works under specific conditions.

The method is restricted to base models rather than instruction-tuned variants that dominate web text generation, the binary F1 score of 0.723 is modest, and the downstream evaluation is underpowered with narrow scope. This feels like a borderline contribution: methodologically rigorous with interesting empirical insights about differential depth responses, yet constrained by its base-model limitation. For a top-tier venue, I'd rate the soundness around 3.5 due to some gaps in the experimental design, excitement at 3 given the interesting task formulation offset by limited applicability, and overall a 3—it's a solid findings paper but the practical restrictions are too significant for a main conference.
```

recursively generated text\"), Introduction §1 para 1, Limitations §8 para 1, Discussion §5 \"Instruct models\"\n- 问题：论文动机围绕 web-crawled text provenance 展开，但全部 6 个 generator 均为 base model。Discussion 明确报告 instruct model 深度信号大幅退化（OLMo-Instruct F1=0.338 vs 0.764 base; Qwen3-0.6B-Instruct near chance binary F1≈0.5）。而 web 上实际部署的 LLM 几乎全部为 instruction-tuned/RLHF 模型。这意味着论文标题和摘要暗示的 \"web-scale provenance characterization\" 应用场景在当前方法下根本不成立。\n- 为什么影响接收：这不是边界局限，而是 **核心方法无法应用于论文自身动机描述的场景**。对主会议而言，一篇以 \"web-crawled corpora\" 为开篇的论文，其核心方法仅对 base model 有效，构成 claims 与 evidence 的根本不匹配 (ARR R4: overclaiming)。\n- 建议修改方向：(1) 大幅修改 framing，将论文明确定位为 \"base-model recursive generation 的实证研究\" 而非 web-scale provenance 工具；(2) 在 Abstract 和 Introduction 中前置 base-model 限制；(3) 或者补充至少 2-3 个主流 instruct model 的实验，即使结果为 negative，也展示方法在真实场景下的 boundary。\n\n**N2** [critical] 下游实验 (§4.4) 严重 underpowered，无法支撑 \"diagnostic not curative\" 的广泛结论\n- 具体位置：§4.4, Appendix I (Table 4), Conclusion §6\n- 问题：\n  - 单一模型 (OLMo-2-0425-1B)、单一任务 (causal LM)、~4K 训练样本、仅 3 个随机种子\n  - 关键 perplexity 差异仅 0.020 (2.367 vs 2.387)，cross-seed std < 0.002\n  - **未报告统计显著性检验**——没有 paired t-test、bootstrap CI、或 effect size for the perplexity difference\n  - 结论 \"depth fingerprinting is diagnostic not curative\" 被写入 Abstract, Conclusion, 和 Contribution #3，但实验只覆盖了一个极小的 corner case\n- 为什么影响接收：将一个 severely underpowered 的单模型实验推广为论文的核心结论之一 (Contribution #3)，违反了 ARR R2 (claims must match evaluation scope)。\n- 建议修改方向：(1) 对 downstream 比较做 bootstrap CI 或 permutation test，报告 p-value 和 effect size；(2) 降级为 \"preliminary negative finding\"，不列为 contribution；(3) 测试至少一个额外下游任务（如 classification 或 QA）和/或一个额外模型。\n\n**N3** [critical] Gen-0 共享种子设计导致 3-class LOGO F1 被系统性高估\n- 具体位置：§3.2 para 2, §4.1, §4.3, Table 1, Table 3\n- 问题：所有 generator 共享相同的 ~1000 Wikipedia seeds 作为 gen-0。在 LOGO 中，held-out generator 的 gen-0 文本与训练集 gen-0 **完全相同**（逐字相同）。这造成直接的 train-test overlap。后果：\n  - gen-0 class F1=0.94（Table 1 注释）是人为虚高的\n  - 3-class LOGO F1=0.789 被系统性高估（因为 gen-0 几乎 \"free\"）\n  - 3-class 数字在论文中被多处显著报告（Table 1 标题、Contribution #2、Conclusion），容易误导读者\n  - 作者确实报告了 binary evaluation (0.723) 作为补充，但 3-class 仍是 Table 1 的主数字\n- 为什么影响接收：数据泄露直接影响 primary evaluation metric 的可信度。虽然 binary metric 部分解决了这个问题，但 3-class 结果仍被突出报告，构成 misleading presentation (ARR R1)。\n- 建议修改方向：(1) 在 Table 1 标题中明确标注 gen-0 overlap caveat；(2) 将 binary F1 设为唯一 primary metric（当前文字上是，但呈现上不是）；(3) 更好的解决方案：为每个 generator 使用独立的 gen-0 seed pool，彻底消除 overlap。\n\n---\n\n## Should-Fix 问题（影响评分但不一票否决）\n\n**W1** [warning] Cross-reference model table (Table 4) 与主表 (Table 1) 数字不一致\n- 具体位置：Table 4 (Pythia-410M LOGO F1 = 0.803) vs Table 1 (6-gen LOGO average = 0.789)\n- 问题：两者都应该用 Pythia-410M 作为 reference model 在 LOGO 下评估，但 F1 差 0.014。可能原因是 Table 4 使用 5-gen LOGO（排除 Qwen3-8B）而 Table 1 使用 6-gen LOGO，但论文未明确说明。类似地，Table 4 中 Qwen3-0.6B reference 的 0.816 与 Discussion 中 \"7-generator split\" 下 Qwen3-0.6B base 的 0.816 数字巧合相同，增加混淆。论文至少涉及 3 种不同的 generator split (5-gen, 6-gen, 7-gen)，但未在每个表格中标注具体 split。\n- 建议：每个表格的 caption 中明确写出使用的 generator split；确保同一 split 下同一 reference model 的结果前后一致。\n\n**W2** [warning] XGBoost 超参不对称的公平性\n- 具体位置：§3.4 para \"Classifier\"\n- 问题：7-feature ensemble 用 200 estimators / max_depth 6，而 baseline detectors 用 100 / 3。虽然 §4.3 末尾报告 sensitivity check 显示 <0.002pp 变化，但这是 **post-hoc justification**。更公正的做法是对每个方法独立调参（如 5-fold CV 选超参）或使用相同设置。当前设计给予 ensemble 更高的 classifier capacity，即使实际影响很小，也在方法论上存在瑕疵。\n- 建议：增加一个 footnote 解释超参选择策略，或在 Appendix 中报告 baseline 在 200/6 设置下的结果。\n\n**W3** [warning] 4-detector + surface ensemble (Table 3) 的表现暴露了贡献的增量性\n- 具体位置：Table 3\n- 问题：当把 4 个现有 detector scores + 2 个 surface features 合并为 6-feature ensemble 后，binary F1 达到 0.724，与本文提出的 7-feature ensemble (0.723) 相当甚至略高。这表明：(1) 现有 detector + 简单 surface features 就能达到同等性能；(2) 本文提出的 NLL 统计量特征（entropy_mean/std/max/min, tail_decay）相对于现有 detector 的增量贡献很小。论文的核心 empirical contribution 因此被部分削弱。\n- 建议：诚实讨论这一结果——当前 Table 3 的 caption 声称 \"the 7-feature ensemble is the strongest single-model method\"，但 4-det+surface 在 3-class (0.805>0.789) 和 binary (0.724≥0.723) 上都不弱于它。核心贡献应重新定位为 **task formulation + differential depth response 的实证发现**，而非 feature engineering 的优越性。\n\n**W4** [warning] Bootstrap CI 细节缺失\n- 具体位置：§3.4 \"Metrics and statistical testing\"\n- 问题：报告了 n=1000 bootstrap resamples 和 95% CI，但未说明 CI 类型（percentile, BCa, or normal）。对于 macro-F1 这种可能存在 skewed distribution 的指标（尤其在 class imbalance 下），percentile 和 BCa 的区别可能显著。另外，bootstrap resamples 是在样本级别还是 document 级别进行的也未说明。\n- 建议：补充一句说明 CI 计算方法。\n\n**W5** [warning] 单一 prompt type 的主实验限制了 differential depth response 声明的普适性\n- 具体位置：§3.2, Appendix B\n- 问题：主实验仅用 continuation prompt (\"Continue the following text\")。Appendix B 的 cross-prompt 实验只用了 Pythia-2.8B 一个 generator。且 elaboration 的 per-depth feature decay rate 仅 1.4%，远低于 continuation 的 16.9%——这暗示 differential depth response 的强度高度依赖 prompt type。cross-generator × cross-prompt 交互效应完全未测试。\n- 建议：至少对 2-3 个 generator 重复 cross-prompt 实验，或者在 main text 中明确声明 differential depth response 的强度是 prompt-dependent 的。\n\n**W6** [warning] \"Differential depth response\" 的机制解释是描述性的而非因果性的\n- 具体位置：§4.2, Discussion §5 \"Shape stationarity\"\n- 问题：论文将 \"location shift in the NLL distribution while preserving functional form\" 作为 mechanistic explanation，但这本质上是对观察到的 pattern 的 restatement 而非因果机制。topic narrowing 被控制（<9% variance），但其他潜在 confound（prompt-induced distributional bias, length-conditional feature behavior 等）未完全排除。Discussion 称 \"We flag this as a hypothesis for future investigation, not a conclusion\"（这句很好），但 Introduction 和 Abstract 的表述更为断言性 (\"consistent with recursive generation inducing a location shift\")。\n- 建议：统一全文表述为假说而非结论。\n\n---\n\n## 小问题（不影响接收）\n\n- **Table 3 caption 不准确**: 声称 \"the 7-feature ensemble is the strongest single-model method on both metrics\"，但 4-det.+surface 在 3-class (0.805) 和 binary (0.724) 上都不低于 7-feature ensemble (0.789, 0.723)。需修改措辞。\n- **样本量不精确**: §3.2 说 \"approximately 1,000 seed passages\" 和 \"approximately 27,000 texts\"，但 6 generators × ~1000 seeds × 5 depths = ~30,000，而 confusion matrix (Table, Appendix F) 显示 N=27,529。差异 ~2,500 样本未解释（可能因 truncated/failed generations），应说明。\n- **tail_decay 引用不精确**: §3.3 将 90th percentile threshold 归因于 \"standard practice in heavy-tail analysis\" 并引用 Hendrycks & Gimpel (2017)，但该论文是 OOD detection baseline，不是 heavy-tail analysis 方法论。引用应更准确。\n- **Figure 1**: 使用 bar chart 展示 Spearman 相关系数，缺少 per-generator variation 信息。建议改用 strip plot 或加 error bars 展示 generator 间变异。\n- **Qwen3-0.6B F1 数字混淆**: Limitations 提到 \"0.816 base under the 7-generator split; base-only yields 0.824, Table 1\"。读者需要理解存在多个不同的 generator split 才能 reconcile 这些数字。建议在首次出现时加括号注释。\n- **引用格式**: bib 中 `self_consuming_mad` 的 first author 在 OpenAlex 中为 Casco-Rodriguez（而非 Alemohammad），`qwen3` 的 first author 为 Yang 而非 Team——均为 OpenAlex 索引差异，不构成错误，但建议核实。\n\n---\n\n## 主要优势\n\n1. **新颖的任务定义**: Ordinal depth estimation 填补了 binary detection 与 recursive generation 分析之间的空白，formalization 清晰、有理论动机。\n2. **严谨的评估协议**: LOGO/LOFO cross-validation 正确地测试了 cross-generator generalization；binary evaluation 解决了 gen-0 leakage（虽然 3-class 呈现仍有问题）。\n3. **Differential depth response 是有意义的实证发现**: 中心趋势特征 monotonic drift + shape 特征 stationary 的分离是一个 clean 的 empirical pattern，对理解 recursive generation 有独立价值。\n4. **诚实报告 negative findings**: instruct model 退化、downstream curation 无效、entropy_min 冗余等 negative results 均被透明报告，展现了良好的学术诚信。\n5. **丰富的消融和鲁棒性分析**: 9 个附录覆盖了 length confound, prompt type, finer-grained depth, QLoRA, LOFO, classifier sensitivity, human-edit robustness, sampling sensitivity, downstream——分析深度超过平均水平。\n6. **Cross-model chain 和 cross-family 实验**: 增强了 real-world relevance 的可信度（尽管仍在 base model 范围内）。\n\n---\n\n## Questions for Authors\n\n1. Per-generator binary F1 (gen-1 vs gen-2+) 的完整分布是什么？当前只报告了 std=0.051，但未给出 range 或每个 generator 的值。\n2. Bootstrap CI 使用的是什么类型（percentile, BCa, normal）？resampling 是在 sample 级别还是 document 级别？\n3. Table 4 (cross-ref) 使用的是几个 generator 的 LOGO？如果是 5-gen，为何不与 6-gen 保持一致？\n4. Wikipedia seed passages 是如何选择的（random, topic-controlled, length-filtered）？selection criteria 会否影响 depth signal 的 generalizability？\n5. Downstream perplexity 差异 (0.020) 的 95% CI 是多少？是否做了统计检验？\n6. 当 Qwen3-0.6B 同时作为 generator 和 reference model 时（Table 4），是否存在 self-referencing bias？该 generator 在该 configuration 下的 held-out F1 是否异常？\n\n---\n\n## Missing References\n\n- **Generative AI text detection under evolving data**: 论文引用了两篇 survey 但未引用近期关于 detection 在 domain/distribution shift 下退化的实证研究，例如 cross-domain detection generalization 方面的工作。\n- **Data mixing for model collapse mitigation**: 论文引用了 Gerstgrasser et al. (2024) 但未讨论其 data mixing strategy 与 depth-aware curation 的关系。\n- **Instruction-tuned text detection**: 缺少对 instruct-model-specific detection challenges 的引用，考虑到论文在 Discussion 中专门讨论了 instruct 退化。\n\n---\n\n## Overall Assessment\n\n**3.0 — Findings acceptance**\n\n这是一篇方法论严谨、分析深入的实证论文，提出了一个有价值的任务定义（ordinal depth estimation）和一个 clean 的实证发现（differential depth response）。论文展现了良好的学术规范：统计测试齐全、negative findings 被透明报告、大量消融实验覆盖了多个 axis of variation。\n\n主要的 concerns 是：\n1. **Framing vs. scope mismatch** (N1): 论文以 web-scale provenance 为动机，但方法仅对 base model 有效，而 instruct model（web 文本的主要来源）接近 chance。这不是一个 \"limitation\"——这是核心 claim 与 evidence 的不匹配。\n2. **Downstream conclusion overreach** (N2): 将一个极其有限的单模型单任务实验推广为核心 contribution。\n3. **3-class metric inflation** (N3): gen-0 train-test overlap 使得被突出报告的 3-class F1 具有误导性。\n\n如果作者能大幅修正 framing（明确定位为 base-model 实证研究而非 web-scale 工具），降级 downstream 结论，并以 binary F1 为唯一 primary metric，本文的核心贡献——任务定义 + differential depth response——足以支撑 Findings 发表。对于主会议接收，则需要在 instruct model 上取得有意义的进展，或展示实际部署场景下的 value。\n\n**置信度: 4/5** — 仔细阅读了全文含全部 9 个附录，核实了关键数字的一致性，在线验证了全部 50 条引用。",

  "project": "gen-depth-fingerprint"
}

[Round 1 审稿请求]:
## 交叉分享 — 请逐条回应 Reviewer 2 的审稿意见

以下是 Reviewer 2 的完整审稿意见。请逐条回应：**[AGREE]** 同意并说明理由，或 **[REBUT]** 反驳并提供证据（cite 具体 Section/Table/Figure）。

特别关注你们意见分歧或互补的地方。

---

## Reviewer 2 完整审稿意见

**Overall**: 3/5 (Findings acceptance)
**一句话总结**: 任务定义新颖且实验方法论扎实，但 base-model-only 的范围限制与论文动机（web-crawled text provenance）之间存在根本性脱节，方法新颖性不足以支撑 main conference acceptance。

### 官方维度打分

| 维度 | 分数 | 理由摘要 |
|------|------|----------|
| Soundness | 3.5/5 | 实验方法严谨（LOGO/LOFO、bootstrap CI、McNemar 检验、Bonferroni 校正、长度混淆控制），但动机与实际范围不匹配 |
| Excitement | 2.5/5 | 任务定义及时有用，但方法全部是标准统计量+XGBoost，differential depth response 本质是已知现象的重新描述 |
| Confidence | 4/5 | 熟悉 AI text detection、OOD detection、model collapse 文献 |
| Reproducibility | 4/5 | 特征定义、超参数、评估协议描述充分；代码承诺发布但未提供 |

### Must-Fix 问题

**N1** [critical] 动机与范围的根本性脱节——base-model-only restriction
- 论文以"web-crawled corpora increasingly contain LLM-generated text"开篇，以"risk-stratified curation decisions"为应用场景。但 web 上的 LLM 文本几乎全部来自 instruction-tuned / RLHF-aligned 模型。论文自己的 instruct 实验表明 depth signal 严重退化：OLMo-Instruct LOGO F1 = 0.338（vs. base 0.764），Qwen3-0.6B length-matched binary F1 ≈ 0.5（chance level）。
- 建议：(a) 重新定位动机为 base-model generation chains 的科学发现；或 (b) 大幅扩展 instruct 实验。

**N2** [critical] 方法新颖性不足以支撑 main conference contribution
- 7 个特征全部是标准统计量（NLL mean/std/max/min/tail + TTR + hapax），分类器是 XGBoost。"Differential depth response"本质上描述的是递归生成引起的 NLL 分布 location shift——这在 OOD detection 和 model collapse 文献中是已知现象。
- 建议：(a) 提供更深入的理论分析；(b) 大幅扩展实验范围。

### Should-Fix 问题

**W1** [warning] 尺度泛化不足：最大生成器仅 8.2B，已出现显著性能下降（held-out F1 = 0.683）。70B+ 模型未测试。

**W2** [warning] Binary F1 = 0.723 的实际效用讨论不足：gen-1 per-class F1 仅 0.64，约 36% 错分率。论文未讨论何种精度水平使 depth fingerprinting 在实践中有意义。

**W3** [warning] Differential depth response 与 model collapse 文献（inference-time vs. training-loop distributional drift）的机制异同未深入讨论。

**W4** [warning] Downstream experiment 规模过小（1 model、1 task、~12K samples）但结论偏宽泛。

**W5** [warning] Gen-0 seed 在所有 generator 间共享导致 LOGO 的 gen-0 train-test overlap，论文未解释不使用独立 seed pool 的原因。

### 小问题
1. Abstract 数字密度过高
2. 正文仅 2 个 Figure，可增加 NLL 分布 violin plot
3. 引用格式：self_consuming_mad 年份/作者待核实；aaronson_watermark 无 venue；strong_model_collapse 年份需核实
4. 特征名 "entropy_*" 实际计算的是 NLL，非信息论 entropy，命名可能引起混淆
5. 缺少数据集基本统计表

### 可能遗漏的引用
1. DetectRL (2024/2025) — 真实场景 benchmark
2. OOD detection 深度文献 — energy-based (Liu et al., NeurIPS 2020), Mahalanobis (Lee et al., NeurIPS 2018)
3. arXiv:2505.11550 (2025) — 最新多类别检测
4. FineWeb/Dolma — 大规模 data curation pipeline

### Questions for Authors
1. 能否提供 instruct 模型 length-matched 条件下的 feature 分布图？
2. Gen-0 seed 为何在所有 generator 间共享？
3. Baseline detectors 是否使用了与 7-feature ensemble 相同的 reference model？
4. Temperature 1.0 下 depth signal 消失——对 greedy/low-temp sampling 是否意味着更强信号？

{
  "text": "## Reviewer 1 交叉回应 — 针对 Reviewer 2 的 Round 1 意见\n\n---\n\n### Must-Fix 问题\n\n**[AGREE] R2-N1: 动机与范围的根本性脱节（base-model-only）**\n\n完全同意。这也是我的 N1。论文 Abstract 首句 \"training corpora increasingly contain recursively generated text of unknown provenance depth\"、Introduction §1 para 1 \"web-crawled corpora\" 反复暗示 web-scale 适用性，但 Discussion §5 \"Instruct models\" 段落报告 OLMo-Instruct F1=0.338、Qwen3-0.6B length-matched binary F1≈0.5，depth signal 在 instruct model 上本质上不存在。R2 的两个建议（重新定位 framing / 扩展 instruct 实验）与我的建议一致。这是两位 reviewer 独立认定的 critical issue。\n\n**[REBUT] R2-N2: 方法新颖性不足**\n\n不同意将此列为 Must-Fix。理由：\n\n1. **论文的核心贡献是 task formulation + empirical characterization，而非 feature engineering。** Introduction §1 para 4 明确写道 \"Our contribution is not a new detection method but the task formulation (ordinal depth estimation) and the finding that combining drifting and stationary features yields consistent cross-generator performance\"（Related Work §2.1 末段也重复了这一定位）。ARR reviewer guidelines (H7) 明确指出不应 penalize methodological simplicity —— \"simple methods solving a new task well\" 是合理的贡献模式。\n\n2. **\"Differential depth response\" 不是已知现象的重新描述。** R2 称其为 \"递归生成引起的 NLL 分布 location shift——OOD detection 和 model collapse 文献中已知\"。但关键区别在于：\n   - Model collapse 文献（Shumailov et al. 2024, §2.2）研究的是 **training-loop** degradation（模型参数在递归训练中变化）；本文研究 **inference chains**（模型参数固定，仅输入递归）。论文 §2.2 明确区分了这两者。\n   - OOD detection 文献描述的是 in-distribution vs. OOD 的 **二值** 区分；本文发现的是 **ordinal** drift pattern，且关键发现是 **drifting vs. stationary features 的分离**（central-tendency drift + shape stationarity）。Figure 1 和 §4.2 的 Spearman 相关系数清楚展示了这一分离：hapax ρ=−0.558, TTR ρ=−0.542, entropy_mean ρ=−0.475（drift）vs. entropy_std |ρ|=0.076, tail_decay |ρ|=0.089（stationary）。\n   - 这一 drift/stationary 分离（shape invariance under recursive generation）在文献中没有 prior documentation。\n\n3. **标准统计量 + XGBoost 恰好证明了 features 而非 classifier 的重要性。** Appendix C (Table) 显示 Random Forest (0.790) 和 Logistic Regression (0.725) 均远超 chance (0.33)，且 per-generator ranking 一致。这正是论文想证明的：discriminative power 在 features 中，不需要复杂模型。\n\n4. **但我同意 R2 的潜在担忧**：论文确实未提供 **理论分析** 解释为何 shape 保持 stationary。Discussion §5 \"Shape stationarity\" 段仅给出了 informal hypothesis（\"shape is determined by the sampling mechanism and architecture-level output distribution—both fixed across generation steps\"），但未提供数学推导或更深入的 empirical decomposition。这是一个合理的 Should-Fix 而非 Must-Fix。\n\n**结论**：R2-N2 作为 Must-Fix 过于严厉。ARR 标准不要求 methodological novelty —— sound empirical contribution + new task formulation 是 Findings-eligible 的。但如果目标是 main conference，我同意需要更深入的 mechanistic analysis。建议降级为 Should-Fix。\n\n---\n\n### Should-Fix 问题\n\n**[AGREE] R2-W1: 尺度泛化不足（最大 8.2B，70B+ 未测试）**\n\n同意，这是一个有效的 limitation。Table 1 显示 Qwen3-8B held-out F1=0.683（CI [0.668, 0.694]），明显低于其他 5 个 ≤2.8B generators 的 0.764–0.856 range。这暗示了一个 clear scaling trend，但当前数据点不足以做 extrapolation。不过我认为这属于 \"acknowledged limitation\" 类别（Limitations §8 para 2 已讨论），不应被 penalize 为 weakness（ARR H16: 不应将已明确承认的 limitation 列为 weakness）。\n\n**[AGREE] R2-W2: Binary F1=0.723 的实际效用未讨论**\n\n同意，且与我的审查补充。gen-1 per-class F1=0.64（§4.1）意味着超过三分之一的 gen-1 文本被错分。论文声称 depth fingerprinting 是 \"diagnostic tool\"，但未讨论在什么 precision/recall 阈值下这个诊断才有意义。例如：如果用于 corpus-level prevalence estimation（而非 document-level classification），F1=0.72 可能足够；如果用于 individual document filtering，则远远不够。这种 use-case-dependent 的效用讨论完全缺失。\n\n**[AGREE] R2-W3: Differential depth response 与 model collapse 的机制异同未深入讨论**\n\n同意。这正是我在 W6 中提到的 \"描述性而非因果性\" 的问题。论文 §2.2 区分了 training-loop vs. inference-chain，但未深入讨论：\n- 两种 distributional drift 的 functional form 是否相同？\n- Model collapse 中 mode dropping 在 inference chains 中是否也发生？\n- Shape stationarity 是否 inference chains 独有？\n\nDiscussion §5 末段仅一句 \"parallels model collapse ... but occurs without parameter updates\"，后接 topic narrowing 控制（<9% variance）。需要更 substantive 的对比分析。\n\n**[AGREE] R2-W4: Downstream experiment 规模过小但结论偏宽泛**\n\n强烈同意。这是我的 N2（Must-Fix），且我比 R2 的评级更严格。R2 将其列为 Should-Fix，但我认为该问题的严重性在于 **downstream conclusion 被列为论文三大 contributions 之一**（Contribution #3, Abstract 末句, Conclusion §6）。如果仅列为 supplementary analysis，Should-Fix 评级合理；但作为核心 contribution，单模型单任务 ~4K 样本 + 无统计检验的实验不达标。\n\n**[AGREE] R2-W5: Gen-0 共享 seed + LOGO overlap**\n\n同意，这是我的 N3。R2 将其列为 Should-Fix，我列为 Must-Fix。关键分歧在于严重性：gen-0 train-test overlap 使得 3-class LOGO F1（论文最显眼的数字之一）被系统性高估，且论文未解释为何不使用独立 seed pool。这不仅是 \"未解释\"，而是 experimental design 层面的疏忽。即使 binary metric 部分解决了问题，3-class 数字仍被 Table 1、Contribution #2、Conclusion 突出呈现。\n\n---\n\n### 小问题\n\n**[AGREE] R2-Minor-1: Abstract 数字密度过高**\n\n同意。Abstract 在 8 行内包含 F1=0.723, CI=[0.717, 0.729], p<0.001, 6 generators, 3 families, 0.6B–8.2B, std=0.051, 3-class F1=0.809, binary F1=0.764 等。ARR 并不禁止，但可读性受损。\n\n**[AGREE] R2-Minor-2: 正文仅 2 个 Figure**\n\n同意。NLL 分布的 violin plot 或 density plot 按 depth 分层会大幅增强 §4.2 (Differential Depth Response) 的直观性。当前仅靠 Figure 1 的 bar chart 展示 Spearman ρ，读者无法直接看到 feature 分布随 depth 的变化。\n\n**[REBUT] R2-Minor-3: 引用格式问题**\n\n部分不同意。我独立验证了全部 50 条引用：\n- `self_consuming_mad`：Alemohammad et al. (ICLR 2024) 是正确的第一作者和年份。OpenAlex 索引中 Casco-Rodriguez 为 first author 是 OpenAlex 的错误，不是论文的错误。\n- `aaronson_watermark`：标注为 \"Technical Talk\" 并附 URL，对于非正式发表的 talk，这种格式是可接受的（虽然可以更规范）。\n- `strong_model_collapse`：Dohmatob et al., ICLR 2025，年份正确。\n- 但我同意 R2 的整体关注——引用格式一致性可以改善。\n\n**[AGREE] R2-Minor-4: \"entropy_*\" 命名可能混淆**\n\n合理的 terminological concern。§3.3 定义这些特征时用 \"token-level negative log-likelihood (NLL)\"，但特征名用 \"entropy_\"。严格说，per-token NLL 不等于信息论 entropy（entropy 是期望 NLL，而非单个 token 的 NLL）。不过这在 NLP 社区是常见的 loose usage，不太可能造成实质混淆。建议加一个 footnote 澄清。\n\n**[AGREE] R2-Minor-5: 缺少数据集基本统计表**\n\n同意。论文缺少一个报告 per-generator, per-depth 样本数、平均长度、长度分布的汇总表。当前散落在各处的 \"approximately 27,000\" 和 confusion matrix N=27,529 之间的 gap 也因此难以 reconcile。\n\n---\n\n### 可能遗漏的引用\n\n**[REBUT] R2-MissingRef-1: DetectRL**\n\n不确定 DetectRL 的具体论文。如果是 real-world benchmark 类工作，可能与本文的 controlled laboratory setting 不直接可比。需要 R2 提供完整引用才能评估。\n\n**[AGREE] R2-MissingRef-2: OOD detection 深度文献（energy-based, Mahalanobis）**\n\n同意。论文引用了 Hendrycks & Gimpel (2017) 作为 OOD baseline，但未引用 energy-based OOD detection (Liu et al., NeurIPS 2020) 和 Mahalanobis distance (Lee et al., NeurIPS 2018)。考虑到 entropy_mean 在功能上等价于 NLL-based OOD score（论文 §3.3 自己承认），引用这些后续工作是合理的。\n\n**[REBUT] R2-MissingRef-3: arXiv:2505.11550**\n\n无法核实此 arxiv ID 的具体内容。R2 应提供标题和作者以便评估相关性。\n\n**[AGREE] R2-MissingRef-4: FineWeb/Dolma 数据 curation pipeline**\n\n同意。论文 §2.4 讨论了 data quality signals 并引用了 DSIR, QuRating, CCNet，但未引用 FineWeb (Penedo et al., 2024) 和 Dolma (Soldaini et al., 2024) 这两个实际大规模 curation pipeline。考虑到论文的 web-crawled provenance 动机，这些引用是重要的。\n\n---\n\n### Questions for Authors 评估\n\n**[AGREE] R2-Q1: instruct 模型 length-matched feature 分布图**\n有价值的请求。当前 Limitations 只给了数字（partial ρ），没有可视化。\n\n**[AGREE] R2-Q2: Gen-0 seed 为何共享**\n这也是我的 N3 中的核心问题。\n\n**[AGREE] R2-Q3: Baseline detectors 是否使用相同 reference model**\n好问题。§3.4 \"Baselines\" 说 Binoculars 和 FastDetectGPT 有各自的 reference/scoring model 定义，但未明确说明是否都用 Pythia-410M。如果 baseline 用了不同的 reference model，比较可能有 confound。\n\n**[REBUT] R2-Q4: Temperature 1.0 下信号消失 → greedy/low-temp 更强？**\n论文 Appendix C (Table, Sampling Sensitivity) 已经回答了这个问题：low temp (0.5) 配置下 binary F1=0.797，default (0.7) 下 0.799，差异仅 0.002pp。所以 greedy/low-temp 并不带来显著更强的信号——是 high temp (1.0) 破坏了信号，而非 low temp 增强了信号。Spearman ρ(entropy_mean) 在 low/default 下为 −0.694/−0.602（都强），仅在 high temp 下坍塌到 −0.002。\n\n---\n\n### 总结：与 R2 的共识与分歧\n\n**共识领域（强）**：\n- N1 base-model framing 问题（两位 reviewer 均列为 critical）\n- W4/W5 downstream underpowered + gen-0 overlap\n- 多处 minor issues（abstract 密度、figure 不足、数据集统计缺失）\n\n**分歧领域**：\n- **N2 方法新颖性**：R2 列为 Must-Fix，我认为应降为 Should-Fix。论文贡献不在于 feature engineering 的新颖性，而在于 task formulation + empirical pattern discovery。ARR H7 明确不应 penalize simplicity。\n- **W5 gen-0 overlap 严重性**：R2 列为 Should-Fix，我列为 Must-Fix（我的 N3），因为它直接影响了论文最显眼的 evaluation metric。\n- **W4 downstream 严重性**：R2 列为 Should-Fix，我列为 Must-Fix（我的 N2），因为 downstream 结论被列为核心 contribution。\n\n**总体评估一致**：两位 reviewer 均给出 3.0 / Findings acceptance，soundness 3.5/5。",
  "reviewer_id": "1",
  "project": "gen-depth-fingerprint"
}

1	% Abstract — Depth Fingerprinting of Recursive LLM Generation Chains
2	\begin{abstract}
3	Recursive application of language models---where each generation step conditions on prior LLM output---produces text with progressively altered distributional properties.
4	We study this phenomenon under controlled conditions, restricting to base (pretrained) models to isolate the generation mechanism from instruction-tuning effects.
5	We formulate \emph{ordinal depth estimation}---classifying text by generation depth rather than a binary real-vs-synthetic label---and characterize a \emph{differential depth response}: central-tendency features drift monotonically with depth while distributional shape features remain near-stationary, consistent with a location shift in the token-level NLL distribution that preserves its functional form.
6	A 7-feature ensemble exploiting this complementarity achieves binary F1\,=\,0.723 for depth discrimination across six generators from three model families, significantly outperforming all individual detection baselines.
7	We additionally report that depth-aware data curation does not improve downstream language model quality over random subsampling at equal sample count.
8	\end{abstract}
9

```text
1	\section*{Limitations}
2	\label{sec:limitations}
3	
4	All six generators are base (pretrained) models.
5	This is a deliberate design choice: base models isolate the generation mechanism from instruction-tuning effects, enabling controlled study of recursive distributional drift.
6	However, web text is predominantly produced by instruction-tuned and RLHF-aligned models.
7	Preliminary instruct experiments confirm that depth signal degrades substantially under instruction tuning: OLMo-2-1B-Instruct drops to LOGO F1\,=\,0.338 (vs.\ 0.764 base) and Qwen3-0.6B with chat template to 0.667 (vs.\ 0.816 under the 7-generator split; base-only yields 0.824, Table~\ref{tab:logo-results}).
8	Length-matched analysis yields near-zero correlations ($|\rho| < 0.02$) and binary classification near chance (F1\,$\approx$\,0.5) for Qwen models, leaving interpretation ambiguous.
9	Extending depth fingerprinting to instruct models requires addressing these confounds---length regulation, format constraints, safety-driven distribution shaping---that instruction tuning introduces.
10	
11	Our experiments include generators up to 8.2B parameters (Qwen3-8B).
12	Depth signal persists at this scale, but cross-scale transfer incurs a moderate gap (held-out F1\,=\,0.683 vs.\ 0.76--0.86 for $\leq$2.8B generators).
13	Frontier models (70B+) remain untested; their higher fluency may further compress feature margins.
14	
15	Differential depth response strength is prompt-type dependent: continuation chains show strongest drift (within-type F1\,=\,0.781) compared to elaboration (0.730), rewriting (0.737), and paraphrastic chains (binary F1\,=\,0.671 vs.\ 0.749 for continuation).
16	DDR is most reliably detected in continuation-style generation; near-paraphrastic settings approach the method's detection floor.
17	Cross-prompt-type transfer (LOPTO F1\,=\,0.753) confirms shared signal, yet additional prompt types (e.g., summarization, instruction following) and cross-generator $\times$ cross-prompt interactions remain untested.
18	
19	Our primary evaluation uses single-generator chains; cross-model chains (\S\ref{sec:results-classification}) confirm depth signal under generator heterogeneity (3-class F1\,=\,0.809, binary F1\,=\,0.764), though the six generators represent a controlled laboratory setting rather than the full diversity of real-world contamination.
20	Simulated human editing (\S\ref{sec:discussion}) suggests graceful degradation, but the gap between simulated token replacement and real-world post-processing remains untested.
21	
22	The reference model is fixed to Pythia-410M throughout, although cross-family reference experiments (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}) confirm the phenomenon is not reference-specific (spread 0.013 across three references).
23	All seed texts are English Wikipedia excerpts; generalization to other languages and domains remains untested.
24	
25	Our default analysis uses fixed decoding parameters (temperature 0.7, nucleus $p{=}0.9$). A sampling ablation (Appendix~\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity}) shows robustness to moderate variation ($t \in [0.5, 0.7]$, ${\leq}0.3$\,pp) but substantial degradation at $t{=}1.0$ ($-6$\,pp), indicating that depth fingerprinting assumes standard decoding regimes.
26	
27	We use 3-class granularity (gen-0\,/\,gen-1\,/\,gen-2+). A 5-class evaluation (Appendix~\ref{app:finer-depth}) yields LOGO F1\,$\approx$\,0.50, with depths 2--4 mutually indistinguishable, validating the 3-class design as the practical resolution limit.
28	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01WH9GyjaQsk23JNxCuBPLyJ`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	Web-crawled corpora increasingly contain LLM-generated text---estimates suggest that 7--17\% of recent scientific papers and peer reviews contain substantial LLM-modified content \cite{mapping_llm_science,monitoring_ai_review}---and this proportion grows as generated text re-enters the web and seeds subsequent generation.
5	The result is recursive generation chains whose outputs progressively diverge from human-written text.
6	Understanding the distributional properties of such recursive generation is a prerequisite for characterizing text provenance, yet the phenomenon itself---how distributional features evolve across generation depths---remains underexplored.
7	
8	We study distributional drift in non-paraphrastic generation chains under controlled conditions, using six base (pretrained) models from three families (Pythia, OLMo, Qwen) spanning multiple scales.
9	We restrict to base models as a deliberate design choice: instruction-tuning introduces confounds---output length variability, format constraints, safety-driven distribution shaping---that obscure the generation mechanism's intrinsic behavior.
10	Preliminary experiments confirm this concern: instruct models show substantially degraded depth signal (\S\ref{sec:discussion}), motivating the controlled base-model setting as the appropriate starting point for isolating recursive drift phenomena.
11	
12	Existing detectors \cite{fast_detectgpt,binoculars,ld_score,detectgpt}, like classical out-of-distribution methods \cite{ood_baseline,energy_ood,mahalanobis_ood}, provide binary real-vs-synthetic classification, conflating qualitatively different material.
13	A first-generation continuation retains much of the source distribution's lexical and structural properties; deeper recursive text exhibits measurably reduced vocabulary diversity and compressed perplexity.
14	We formulate \emph{ordinal depth estimation}---classifying text into generation depth classes (gen-0\,/\,gen-1\,/\,gen-2+) rather than producing a binary label---and refer to the overall framework as \emph{depth fingerprinting}.
15	No existing method addresses this task in non-paraphrastic continuation chains.
16	When repurposed as ordinal depth predictors, individual detection scores exhibit high cross-generator variance (binary F1 from 0.52 to 0.71; \S\ref{sec:results-baselines}), making any single detector unreliable.
17	Concurrent work on iterative paraphrasing chains \cite{broken_telephone,markovian_chains} analyzes convergence under semantic-preservation constraints; we study non-paraphrastic chains where the absence of semantic anchoring permits richer depth signal.
18	
19	We characterize a \emph{differential depth response}: central-tendency and diversity features---including reference-model mean entropy, type-token ratio, and hapax ratio---drift monotonically with generation depth, while distributional shape features (entropy standard deviation, tail decay) remain near-stationary.
20	We hypothesize that recursive generation induces a location shift in the NLL distribution while preserving its functional form; under this account, the sampling mechanism governs distributional shape regardless of input content.
21	The combination of drifting and stationary features yields a 7-feature ensemble that achieves binary F1\,=\,0.723 for leak-free depth discrimination (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+).
22	However, the strength of this signal is prompt-type dependent: continuation chains exhibit stronger drift than elaboration or rewriting chains (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
23	
24	We additionally report a negative finding: despite the effectiveness of quality-based data curation for pretraining \cite{fineweb,dolma}, depth-aware curation does not improve downstream language model quality compared to random subsampling at equal sample count (perplexity: 2.367 vs.\ 2.387).
25	
26	Our contributions are:
27	\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
28	    \item \textbf{Task formulation.} We define ordinal generation depth estimation as a task beyond binary detection, providing a framework for characterizing how distributional properties evolve across recursive generation depths.
29	    \item \textbf{Empirical characterization.} We document differential depth response across six base-model generators spanning three model families: a 7-feature ensemble combining drifting and stationary features achieves binary F1\,=\,0.723 for leak-free depth discrimination, outperforming all individual detection baselines ($p < 0.001$).
30	\end{enumerate}
31	
32	The remainder of this paper is organized as follows.
33	\S\ref{sec:related} surveys related work on text detection, model collapse, and data quality.
34	\S\ref{sec:method} describes generation chain construction, feature extraction, and evaluation protocol.
35	\S\ref{sec:results} presents classification results, differential depth response analysis, and baseline comparisons.
36	\S\ref{sec:discussion} discusses mechanistic hypotheses and scope boundaries.
37	
```

      "content": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nWeb-crawled corpora increasingly contain LLM-generated text---estimates suggest that 7--17\\% of recent scientific papers and peer reviews contain substantial LLM-modified content \\cite{mapping_llm_science,monitoring_ai_review}---and this proportion grows as generated text re-enters the web and seeds subsequent generation.\nThe result is recursive generation chains whose outputs progressively diverge from human-written text.\nUnderstanding the distributional properties of such recursive generation is a prerequisite for characterizing text provenance, yet the phenomenon itself---how distributional features evolve across generation depths---remains underexplored.\n\nWe study distributional drift in non-paraphrastic generation chains under controlled conditions, using six base (pretrained) models from three families (Pythia, OLMo, Qwen) spanning multiple scales.\nWe restrict to base models as a deliberate design choice: instruction-tuning introduces confounds---output length variability, format constraints, safety-driven distribution shaping---that obscure the generation mechanism's intrinsic behavior.\nPreliminary experiments confirm this concern: instruct models show substantially degraded depth signal (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}), motivating the controlled base-model setting as the appropriate starting point for isolating recursive drift phenomena.\n\nExisting detectors \\cite{fast_detectgpt,binoculars,ld_score,detectgpt}, like classical out-of-distribution methods \\cite{ood_baseline,energy_ood,mahalanobis_ood}, provide binary real-vs-synthetic classification, conflating qualitatively different material.\nA first-generation continuation retains much of the source distribution's lexical and structural properties; ```text
1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\subsection{Task Formulation}
5	\label{sec:task}
6	
7	Given a text $x$, the goal is to predict its \emph{generation depth} $d \in \{0, 1, 2{+}\}$, where $d{=}0$ denotes human-written text, $d{=}1$ denotes first-generation LLM output conditioned on human text, and $d{=}2{+}$ denotes text produced by recursively conditioning an LLM on its own prior output at least twice.
8	Formally, let $\mathcal{C}_0$ be a reference corpus of human-written text.
9	A generation chain of depth $k$ is defined recursively: $x^{(0)} \sim \mathcal{C}_0$, and $x^{(k)} = G(x^{(k-1)})$ for $k \geq 1$, where $G$ denotes stochastic LLM generation conditioned on its input.
10	We frame depth estimation as an ordinal classification task over three classes rather than binary real-vs-synthetic detection, enabling finer-grained provenance characterization.
11	Depths beyond 2 are merged into a single $2{+}$ class, consistent with monotonic feature convergence that compresses inter-class margins beyond depth 2 (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}).
12	
13	\subsection{Generation Chain Construction}
14	\label{sec:generation}
15	
16	We construct generation chains using six generators spanning three model families: \textbf{Pythia} (2.8B, 1B) \cite{pythia}, \textbf{OLMo} (OLMo-2-1B) \cite{olmo}, and \textbf{Qwen} (Qwen3-1.7B, Qwen3-0.6B, Qwen3-8B-Base) \cite{qwen3}.
17	This selection covers parameter scales from 0.6B to 8.2B and diverse pretraining corpora, enabling evaluation of cross-generator and cross-scale generalization.
18	
19	For each generator, we sample approximately 1{,}000 seed passages from English Wikipedia as $\mathcal{C}_0$.
20	The same pool of seed passages serves as gen-0 for all generators.
21	In LOGO evaluation, gen-0 samples from the held-out generator's chains also appear (with the same text) in the training generators' chains, creating a potential train--test overlap for the gen-0 class.
22	We address this with a supplementary binary evaluation excluding gen-0 entirely (\S\ref{sec:results-baselines}).
23	Each seed is iteratively fed through the generator to produce a chain of depth 4 (i.e., gen-0 through gen-4), using the prompt ``\texttt{Continue the following text: \{text\}}''.
24	All generation uses temperature $\tau{=}0.7$, nucleus sampling with $p{=}0.9$, and maximum length of 256 new tokens.
25	The theoretical yield is $6 \times {\sim}1{,}000 \times 5 \approx 30{,}000$ texts; after removing generation failures, exact duplicates, and passages shorter than 50 tokens, 27{,}529 texts remain.
26	For classification, depths 2--4 are collapsed into the $2{+}$ class.
27	
28	\subsection{Feature Extraction}
29	\label{sec:features}
30	
31	The seven features are motivated by two complementary views of distributional drift.
32	Reference-model features capture how the text's token-level surprise distribution shifts relative to a fixed model; surface features capture how lexical diversity contracts under recursive generation.
33	Together, they span both the generative mechanism (what the model produces) and the surface manifestation (how the vocabulary changes).
34	This two-pronged design is not post-hoc: it follows directly from the hypothesis that recursive generation induces a location shift in the NLL distribution (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}).
35	
36	We extract seven distributional features per text, organized into two groups based on what they measure rather than how they are computed.
37	
38	\paragraph{Reference-model features.}
39	A held-out reference model (Pythia-410M, chosen to be smaller than all generators) computes per-token negative log-likelihood (NLL) for each text.
40	Although Pythia-410M shares the GPT-NeoX architecture with two of the six generators, cross-family reference model experiments (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}) confirm that depth features are not reference-specific (LOGO F1 varies by only 0.013 across Pythia, OLMo, and Qwen references).
41	From the resulting NLL sequence, we extract five statistics:
42	\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
43	    \item \textbf{entropy\_mean}\footnote{We use ``entropy'' as shorthand for token-level negative log-likelihood under the reference model, following convention in prior detection work. These are point-wise NLL values, not Shannon entropy of the full distribution.}: mean token-level NLL, capturing the average predictability of the text under the reference model;
44	    \item \textbf{entropy\_std}: standard deviation of token-level NLL, measuring the spread of per-token surprise;
45	    \item \textbf{entropy\_max}: maximum token-level NLL, capturing the most surprising token;
46	    \item \textbf{entropy\_min}: minimum token-level NLL, capturing the most predictable token---as generation depth increases, the floor of token-level surprise drops, reflecting the model's increasing confidence on its own outputs.
47	    Although entropy\_min shows near-zero marginal impact in leave-one-feature-out analysis ($\Delta$F1\,=\,+0.01\,pp), we retain it for completeness of the NLL summary statistics (mean, std, max, min) and because removing it does not improve performance;
48	    \item \textbf{tail\_decay}: proportion of tokens whose NLL exceeds the 90th percentile of the reference distribution (computed over $\mathcal{C}_0$), measuring how heavy-tailed the NLL distribution is relative to human text.
49	    Sensitivity analysis confirms stable classification (F1 variation ${<}$0.3\,pp) across thresholds from the 85th to 95th percentile.
50	\end{itemize}
51	
52	\paragraph{Surface features.}
53	Two features capture lexical diversity without requiring a reference model:
54	\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
55	    \item \textbf{TTR}: type-token ratio (number of unique word types divided by total tokens);
56	    \item \textbf{hapax\_ratio}: proportion of words appearing exactly once (\emph{hapax legomena}).
57	\end{itemize}
58	
59	\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}
60	The grouping reflects an empirical pattern we term \emph{differential depth response}: central-tendency and diversity features (entropy\_mean, TTR, hapax\_ratio) drift monotonically with generation depth, while distributional shape features (entropy\_std, tail\_decay) remain near-stationary.
61	This differential behavior---consistent with recursive generation inducing a location shift in the NLL distribution while preserving its functional form---motivates combining both feature types.
62	As shown in \S\ref{sec:results}, the full 7-feature set significantly outperforms either group alone.
63	
64	\subsection{Evaluation Protocol}
65	\label{sec:evaluation}
66	
67	\paragraph{Cross-validation.}
68	To evaluate generalization to unseen generators, we use \textbf{Leave-One-Generator-Out (LOGO)} cross-validation: in each fold, one of the six generators is held out for testing and the remaining five provide training data.
69	We additionally report \textbf{Leave-One-Family-Out (LOFO)} results, where an entire model family (e.g., all Qwen models) is held out, testing generalization across architectural lineages.
70	
71	\paragraph{Classifier.}
72	We use XGBoost \cite{xgboost} as the classifier throughout.
73	Gradient-boosted trees handle heterogeneous feature scales and nonlinear interactions without extensive hyperparameter tuning, and their feature importance scores support interpretability.
74	We use $n\_\text{estimators}{=}200$, $\max\_\text{depth}{=}6$, and learning rate $0.1$ for the 7-feature ensemble.
75	Baseline detectors (\S\ref{sec:results-baselines}) use the same XGBoost framework with $n\_\text{estimators}{=}100$ and $\max\_\text{depth}{=}3$ to avoid overfitting their single-feature input.
76	LOGO F1 is stable across random seeds (mean\,=\,0.789, std\,=\,0.0009 over 5 seeds), confirming that results are not sensitive to classifier initialization.
77	Alternative classifiers (random forest, logistic regression) yield comparable performance (Appendix~\ref{app:classifier}), confirming that discriminative power resides in the features, not the classifier.
78	We evaluated two ordinal-specific methods (CORAL \cite{coral_ordinal}, Frank-Hall ordinal ensemble \cite{frank_hall_ordinal}); neither significantly outperforms multiclass XGBoost (3-class LOGO F1 0.771--0.793 vs.\ 0.789, CIs overlapping), confirming that with only three ordinal levels, ordinal structure provides minimal additional benefit.
79	
80	\paragraph{Metrics and statistical testing.}
81	The primary metric is \textbf{macro-averaged F1} over the three depth classes, which weights minority classes (gen-1, gen-2+) equally against the typically easier gen-0 class.
82	We report per-class F1 scores and 95\% confidence intervals via \emph{percentile bootstrap} ($n{=}1{,}000$), resampling at the document level within each held-out fold.
83	For pairwise model comparisons (e.g., full features vs.\ ablated subsets), we use the \textbf{McNemar test} on disagreement tables.
84	For comparisons against detection baselines, we use \textbf{paired bootstrap} significance tests.
85	All reported $p$-values are two-sided.
86	We report six pairwise comparisons in \S\ref{sec:results-baselines}; applying Bonferroni correction ($\alpha_{\text{adj}} = 0.05/6 = 0.0083$), all remain significant at $p < 0.001$.
87	Feature--depth correlations (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}) are computed on class-balanced subsamples (equal samples per depth class) to control for depth-class imbalance in the generation pipeline.
88	
89	\paragraph{Baselines.}
90	We compare the 7-feature ensemble against four single-score detection methods repurposed as ordinal depth predictors: Avg-LogProb, FastDetectGPT \cite{fast_detectgpt}, Binoculars \cite{binoculars}, and LD-Score \cite{ld_score}.
91	Each baseline score is used as a univariate feature in the same XGBoost framework under LOGO cross-validation, ensuring a controlled comparison.
92	
93	\paragraph{Length confound control.}
94	Since TTR and hapax\_ratio are mechanically length-dependent, we verify that depth signal is not an artifact of systematic length variation.
95	We conduct three complementary analyses: (1) Spearman correlation between text length and each feature (requiring $|\rho| < 0.15$ for non-confounded features), (2) classification with length-normalized features, and (3) comparison on length-matched subsets.
96	
```

seeds), confirming that results are not sensitive to classifier initialization.\nAlternative classifiers (random forest, logistic regression) yield comparable performance (Appendix~\\ref{app:classifier}), confirming that discriminative power resides in the features, not the classifier.\nWe evaluated two ordinal-specific methods (CORAL \\cite{coral_ordinal}, Frank-Hall ordinal ensemble \\cite{frank_hall_ordinal}); neither significantly outperforms multiclass XGBoost (3-class LOGO F1 0.771--0.793 vs.\\ 0.789, CIs overlapping), confirming that with only three ordinal levels, ordinal structure provides minimal additional benefit.\n\n\\paragraph{Metrics and statistical testing.}\nThe primary metric is \\textbf{macro-averaged F1} over the three depth classes, which weights minority classes (gen-1, gen-2+) equally against the typically easier gen-0 class.\nWe report per-class F1 scores and 95\\% confidence intervals via \\emph{percentile bootstrap} ($n{=}1{,}000$), resampling at the document level within each held-out fold.\nFor pairwise model comparisons (e.g., full features vs.\\ ablated subsets), we use the \\textbf{McNemar test} on disagreement tables.\nFor comparisons against detection baselines, we use \\textbf{paired bootstrap} significance tests.\nAll reported $p$-values are two-sided.\nWe report six pairwise comparisons in \\S\\ref{sec:results-baselines}; applying Bonferroni correction ($\\alpha_{\\text{adj}} = 0.05/6 = 0.0083$), all remain significant at $p < 0.001$.\nFeature--depth correlations (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}) are computed on class-balanced subsamples (equal samples per depth class) to control for depth-class imbalance in the generation pipeline.\n\n\\paragraph{Baselines.}\nWe compare the 7-feature ensemble against four single-score detection methods repurposed as ordinal depth predictors: Avg-LogProb, FastDetectGPT \\cite{fast_detectgpt}, Binoculars \\cite{binoculars}, and LD-Score \\cite{ld_score}.\nEach baseline score is used as a univariate feature in the same XGBoost framework under LOGO cross-validation, ensuring a controlled comparison.\n\n\\paragraph{Length confound control.}\nSince TTR and hapax\\_ratio are mechanically length-dependent, we verify that depth signal is not an artifact of systematic length variation.\nWe conduct three complementary analyses: (1) Spearman correlation between text length and each feature (requiring $|\\rho| < 0.15$ for non-confounded features), (2) classification with length-normalized features, and (3) comparison on length-matched subsets.\n",

```text
1	\section{Results}
2	\label{sec:results}
3	
4	We evaluate depth fingerprinting along four axes: cross-generator classification (\S\ref{sec:results-classification}), differential depth response of individual features (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}), comparison with existing detection baselines (\S\ref{sec:results-baselines}), and downstream utility for data curation (\S\ref{sec:results-downstream}).
5	
6	\subsection{Depth Classification Performance}
7	\label{sec:results-classification}
8	
9	Table~\ref{tab:logo-results} reports leave-one-generator-out (LOGO) macro-F1 for 3-class depth estimation (gen-0\,/\,gen-1\,/\,gen-2+) across six generators spanning three model families and parameter counts from 0.6B to 8.2B.
10	
11	The 7-feature ensemble achieves 3-class LOGO F1\,=\,0.789 (Table~\ref{tab:logo-results}). Because gen-0 seed texts are shared across generators (\S\ref{sec:generation}), the gen-0 class F1 (0.94) may be inflated; we therefore report a leak-free binary evaluation (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+, F1\,=\,0.723) as the primary metric in \S\ref{sec:results-baselines}.
12	Per-generator held-out F1 ranges from 0.683 (Qwen3-8B) to 0.856 (Pythia-1B).
13	Per-class F1 varies by depth: gen-0\,=\,0.94, gen-2+\,=\,0.80, gen-1\,=\,0.64; the primary difficulty lies in distinguishing gen-1 from gen-2+, where feature margins narrow.
14	
15	\paragraph{Cross-scale transfer.}
16	The six-generator evaluation includes Qwen3-8B (8.2B parameters), confirming that depth signal persists beyond the $\leq$2.8B training generators, though with a moderate gap: Qwen3-8B held-out F1\,=\,0.683 (CI [0.668, 0.694]) vs.\ 0.76--0.86 for smaller generators.
17	Adding Qwen3-8B data improves classification on the original five generators (+0.8\,pp to 0.810); a volume-matched control confirms 94\% of this gain is attributable to generator diversity rather than data quantity.
18	
19	\paragraph{Cross-family generalization.}
20	Leave-one-family-out (LOFO) evaluation---holding out an entire model family and training on the remaining two---yields macro-F1 of 0.772 (Pythia held out), 0.762 (OLMo held out), and 0.754 (Qwen held out), confirming that depth features transfer across architectural families with only modest degradation (average $\Delta$\,=\,$-$2.6\,pp vs.\ LOGO).
21	
22	\paragraph{Cross-model chains.}
23	Cross-model chains---where each generation step uses a randomly selected generator---yield 3-class F1\,=\,0.809 and binary F1\,=\,0.764 under 5-fold stratified cross-validation, confirming that depth signal persists under generator heterogeneity.
24	
25	\paragraph{Length confound control.}
26	Text length correlates weakly with depth (Spearman $|\rho| < 0.14$).
27	Length-normalized feature trends are preserved, and length-matched subsampling yields comparable or higher F1 ($\Delta$\,=\,+0.041), ruling out length as a confound (Appendix~\ref{app:length}).
28	
29	
30	\begin{table}[t]
31	\centering
32	\small
33	\begin{tabular}{llcccc}
34	\toprule
35	\textbf{Generator} & \textbf{Family} & \textbf{Params} & \textbf{3-class F1} & \textbf{Binary F1} & \textbf{95\% CI} \\
36	\midrule
37	Pythia-1B       & Pythia & 1.0B & 0.856 & \textbf{0.795} & [0.780, 0.809] \\
38	Pythia-2.8B     & Pythia & 2.8B & 0.804 & \textbf{0.723} & [0.709, 0.739] \\
39	OLMo-2-1B      & OLMo   & 1.5B & 0.764 & \textbf{0.677} & [0.660, 0.692] \\
40	Qwen3-0.6B     & Qwen   & 0.6B & 0.824 & \textbf{0.756} & [0.742, 0.772] \\
41	Qwen3-1.7B     & Qwen   & 1.7B & 0.806 & \textbf{0.748} & [0.732, 0.762] \\
42	Qwen3-8B       & Qwen   & 8.2B & 0.683 & \textbf{0.641} & [0.625, 0.657] \\
43	\midrule
44	\multicolumn{3}{l}{Average (6-gen LOGO)} & 0.789 & \textbf{0.723} & --- \\
45	\multicolumn{3}{l}{5-gen LOGO (excl.\ Qwen3-8B)} & 0.802 & \textbf{0.740} & --- \\
46	\bottomrule
47	\end{tabular}
48	\caption{6-generator LOGO macro-F1 for the 7-feature ensemble (bootstrap 95\% CIs, $n$\,=\,1000). Gen-0 samples are shared across generators (\S\ref{sec:generation}); 3-class results are supplementary. Binary F1 (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+) is the primary metric.}
49	\label{tab:logo-results}
50	\end{table}
51	
52	\paragraph{Ordinal regression comparison.}
53	CORAL ordinal regression \cite{coral_ordinal} and Frank-Hall ordinal reduction \cite{frank_hall_ordinal} achieve 3-class F1 of 0.771 and 0.793 respectively, with all CIs overlapping the standard XGBoost (0.789).
54	Explicitly encoding ordinal structure does not yield a measurable gain, likely because 3-class granularity provides insufficient ordinal resolution for ranking-aware losses.
55	
56	
57	\subsection{Differential Depth Response}
58	\label{sec:results-differential}
59	
60	Features exhibit \emph{differential depth response}: central-tendency and diversity measures drift monotonically with depth, while distributional shape measures remain near-stationary.
61	This pattern is consistent with recursive generation inducing a location shift in the token-level NLL distribution---the text becomes progressively more predictable (mean shifts), while the functional form of the distribution is preserved (shape is invariant).
62	
63	\begin{figure}[t]
64	\centering
65	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig1_differential_response.pdf}
66	\caption{Differential depth response: Spearman correlations between individual features and generation depth. Central-tendency and diversity features (hapax ratio, TTR, entropy mean) show strong monotonic drift, while shape features (entropy std, tail decay) remain near-stationary.}
67	\label{fig:feature-drift}
68	\end{figure}
69	
70	Individual-level Spearman correlations with depth confirm the split (Figure~\ref{fig:feature-drift}):
71	drifting features show strong monotonic trends (hapax ratio $\rho = -0.558$, TTR $\rho = -0.542$, entropy mean $\rho = -0.475$),
72	while shape features show weak or negligible correlations (entropy std $|\rho| = 0.076$, tail decay $|\rho| = 0.089$).
73	However, these correlations are computed on continuation chains; elaboration and rewriting chains show substantially weaker drift (Appendix~\ref{app:prompt-type}), and this prompt-type dependence bounds the generality of the differential response characterization.
74	
75	\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}
76	Table~\ref{tab:ablation} reports ablation results.
77	The full 7-feature set (F1\,=\,0.789) significantly outperforms both surface-only features (TTR, hapax\_ratio; F1\,=\,0.729) and reference-only features (entropy mean/std/max/min, tail\_decay; F1\,=\,0.745; McNemar $p \approx 0$).
78	Permutation importance ranks hapax ratio highest (0.269), followed by entropy mean (0.164) and TTR (0.159).
79	Surface features capture vocabulary impoverishment; reference features capture distributional shift---neither subsumes the other.
80	
81	\paragraph{Cross-family reference models.}
82	To confirm that differential depth response is not an artifact of the Pythia-410M reference model, we recompute all features using two cross-family references: OLMo-2-1B (1.5B) and Qwen3-0.6B.
83	All three reference models achieve comparable LOGO F1: Pythia-410M\,=\,0.803, OLMo-2-1B\,=\,0.807, Qwen3-0.6B\,=\,0.816 (spread 0.013; Table~\ref{tab:cross-ref}).
84	Entropy drift direction is consistently negative across all reference models (Table~\ref{tab:cross-ref}), confirming that the differential depth response is a property of recursive generation, not of the specific reference model used.
85	
86	\input{table4}
87	
88	
89	\begin{table}[t]
90	\centering
91	\small
92	\begin{tabular}{lcc}
93	\toprule
94	\textbf{Feature set} & \textbf{LOGO F1} & \textbf{Top feature (importance)} \\
95	\midrule
96	Full (7 features)     & 0.789 & hapax\_ratio (0.269) \\
97	Surface only (2)      & 0.729 & hapax\_ratio (0.320) \\
98	Reference only (5)    & 0.745 & entropy\_mean (0.218) \\
99	\bottomrule
100	\end{tabular}
101	\caption{Feature ablation (6-gen LOGO). Full $>$ surface = reference confirms complementarity between surface diversity features and reference-model distributional features.}
102	\label{tab:ablation}
103	\end{table}
104	
105	Leave-one-feature-out analysis confirms that 6 of 7 features contribute positively, with entropy\_max showing the largest marginal impact despite modest split frequency (Appendix~\ref{app:lofo}).
106	
107	
108	\subsection{Comparison with Detection Baselines}
109	\label{sec:results-baselines}
110	
111	\begin{figure}[t]
112	\centering
113	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig3_baseline_comparison.png}
114	\caption{LOGO macro-F1 with 95\% bootstrap confidence intervals for each detection baseline and the 7-feature ensemble. Per-generator held-out F1 varies more for single detectors than for the ensemble.}
115	\label{fig:baseline-ci}
116	\end{figure}
117	
118	We repurpose four existing detection scores as ordinal depth predictors by training the same classifier on each score as a single feature, using identical LOGO evaluation.
119	Because gen-0 seed texts are shared across generators (\S\ref{sec:generation}), 3-class LOGO evaluation creates a train--test overlap for the gen-0 class.
120	We therefore report both 3-class and binary (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+) results; the binary metric is the primary measure of depth discrimination (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}).
121	
122	\begin{table}[t]
123	\centering
124	\small
125	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
126	\toprule
127	\textbf{Method} & \textbf{3-class F1} & \textbf{Binary F1} & \textbf{Per-gen std} \\
128	\midrule
129	7-feature ensemble    & 0.789 & \textbf{0.723} & 0.051 \\
130	4-det.\ + surface     & \textbf{0.805} & 0.724 & 0.049 \\
131	4-detector ens.\      & 0.796 & 0.712 & 0.044 \\
132	Avg-LogProb           & 0.737 & 0.712 & 0.044 \\
133	LD-Score              & 0.655 & 0.701 & 0.045 \\
134	FastDetectGPT         & 0.688 & 0.616 & 0.056 \\
135	Binoculars            & 0.417 & 0.524 & 0.022 \\
136	\bottomrule
137	\end{tabular}
138	\caption{Depth classification under unified 6-gen LOGO evaluation. \textbf{3-class}: gen-0/gen-1/gen-2+ (gen-0 samples shared across generators; see \S\ref{sec:generation}). \textbf{Binary}: gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+ only, eliminating the gen-0 train--test overlap. Per-gen std measures cross-generator consistency of the binary metric. The 7-feature ensemble matches the multi-detector ensemble on binary F1 (0.723 vs.\ 0.724); the contribution is simplicity (7 summary statistics from one reference model) rather than performance superiority.}
139	\label{tab:baselines}
140	\end{table}
141	
142	The 7-feature ensemble achieves binary F1\,=\,0.723, outperforming every individual baseline ($p < 0.001$ for all; Table~\ref{tab:baselines}).
143	Combining all four detection scores into a 4-detector ensemble yields binary F1\,=\,0.712---below the 7-feature ensemble; adding surface features (TTR, hapax ratio) raises it to 0.724, matching the 7-feature ensemble and confirming that surface diversity features capture signal complementary to detection scores.
144	The multi-detector 3-class advantage (0.796 vs.\ 0.789) is driven entirely by gen-0 classification; existing detectors do not capture the finer depth signal between gen-1 and gen-2+.
145	
146	
147	\subsection{Downstream Utility}
148	\label{sec:results-downstream}
149	
150	Depth-aware data curation does not improve downstream language model quality.
151	Fine-tuning OLMo-2-0425-1B on five data variants (3 seeds each) shows that data quantity dominates depth composition: random subsampling (ppl\,=\,2.367) outperforms depth-stratified selection (ppl\,=\,2.387) at equal sample count (Appendix~\ref{app:downstream}).
152

Table~\\ref{tab:cross-ref}).\nEntropy drift direction is consistently negative across all reference models (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-ref}), confirming that the differential depth response is a property of recursive generation, not of the specific reference model used.\n\n\\input{table4}\n\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Feature set} & \\textbf{LOGO F1} & \\textbf{Top feature (importance)} \\\\\n\\midrule\nFull (7 features)     & 0.789 & hapax\\_ratio (0.269) \\\\\nSurface only (2)      & 0.729 & hapax\\_ratio (0.320) \\\\\nReference only (5)    & 0.745 & entropy\\_mean (0.218) \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Feature ablation (6-gen LOGO). Full $>$ surface = reference confirms complementarity between surface diversity features and reference-model distributional features.}\n\\label{tab:ablation}\n\\end{table}\n\nLeave-one-feature-out analysis confirms that 6 of 7 features contribute positively, with entropy\\_max showing the largest marginal impact despite modest split frequency (Appendix~\\ref{app:lofo}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Comparison with Detection Baselines}\n\\label{sec:results-baselines}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig3_baseline_comparison.png}\n\\caption{LOGO macro-F1 with 95\\% bootstrap confidence intervals for each detection baseline and the 7-feature ensemble. Per-generator held-out F1 varies more for single detectors than for the ensemble.}\n\\label{fig:baseline-ci}\n\\end{figure}\n\nWe repurpose four existing detection scores as ordinal depth predictors by training the same classifier on each score as a single feature, using identical LOGO evaluation.\nBecause gen-0 seed texts are shared across generators (\\S\\ref{sec:generation}), 3-class LOGO evaluation creates a train--test overlap for the gen-0 class.\nWe therefore report both 3-class and binary (gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+) results; the binary metric is the primary measure of depth discrimination (Table~\\ref{tab:baselines}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{3-class F1} & \\textbf{Binary F1} & \\textbf{Per-gen std} \\\\\n\\midrule\n7-feature ensemble    & 0.789 & \\textbf{0.723} & 0.051 \\\\\n4-det.\\ + surface     & \\textbf{0.805} & 0.724 & 0.049 \\\\\n4-detector ens.\\      & 0.796 & 0.712 & 0.044 \\\\\nAvg-LogProb           & 0.737 & 0.712 & 0.044 \\\\\nLD-Score              & 0.655 & 0.701 & 0.045 \\\\\nFastDetectGPT         & 0.688 & 0.616 & 0.056 \\\\\nBinoculars            & 0.417 & 0.524 & 0.022 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Depth classification under unified 6-gen LOGO evaluation. \\textbf{3-class}: gen-0/gen-1/gen-2+ (gen-0 samples shared across generators; see \\S\\ref{sec:generation}). \\textbf{Binary}: gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+ only, eliminating the gen-0 train--test overlap. Per-gen std measures cross-generator consistency of the binary metric. The 7-feature ensemble matches the multi-detector ensemble on binary F1 (0.723 vs.\\ 0.724); the contribution is simplicity (7 summary statistics from one reference model) rather than performance superiority.}\n\\label{tab:baselines}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe 7-feature ensemble achieves binary F1\\,=\\,0.723, outperforming every individual baseline ($p < 0.001$ for all; Table~\\ref{tab:baselines}).\nCombining all four detection scores into a 4-detector ensemble yields binary F1\\,=\\,0.712---below the 7-feature ensemble; adding surface features (TTR, hapax ratio) raises it to 0.724, matching the 7-feature ensemble and confirming that surface diversity features capture signal complementary to detection scores.\nThe multi-detector 3-class advantage (0.796 vs.\\ 0.789) is driven entirely by gen-0 classification; existing detectors do not capture the finer depth signal between gen-1 and gen-2+.\n\n\n\\subsection{Downstream Utility}\n\\label{sec:results-downstream}\n\nDepth-aware data curation does not improve downstream language model quality.\nFine-tuning OLMo-2-0425-1B on five data variants (3 seeds each) shows that data quantity dominates depth composition: random subsampling (ppl\\,=\\,2.367) outperforms depth-stratified selection (ppl\\,=\\,2.387) at equal sample count (Appendix~\\ref{app:downstream}).\n",
